```text
This document is an email thread about a **technical design/specification review** for the **McLane Jazz Project**, focused on how to attach **vertical sprinkler pipes** to the Exotec racking system. It is not a contract or formal report, but rather an engineering coordination email chain between **Alleato Group**, **Exotec**, and **Hy-Tek** discussing whether the proposed pipe support method is acceptable and how it should be implemented safely and within system constraints.

The main topic is a proposal to use **three vertical pipes instead of one** and to mount them using **struts / pipe clamps** attached to the **uprights** of the rack. Several technical constraints are discussed: Exotec notes that the **upright holes are 9 mm**, so **1/2-inch bolts are not compatible**, and the **front upright holes are hard to access** because of the robot arm path. Earlier in the thread, Kebba Mass states that the installation plan uses the **holes on the flange of the uprights** and that the **strut will be outside the system and not obstruct operation**. The pipe sizes and load details are also specified: **horizontal pipes are 1.5 inches**, **vertical pipes are 2 inches**, and the additional load is stated as **154 pounds per vertical pipe with water**. Kebba also notes bolt dimensions of **1/2-inch diameter, 1.5 inches long** and gives intended installation heights of **11'-0"**, **20'-6"**, and **30'-3" from the floor**.

Exotec’s response raises a structural concern that the **vertical sprinkler support must be at the same height as the horizontal beam** per the referenced **043477-B SPRINKLER INTEGRATION RACK II** detail views (**D7 and D8**), because placing it higher could create a **bending moment** on the upright. Exotec also clarifies that the racking system was originally designed to accept **2 vertical 3-inch pipes**, so the proposed **3 vertical 2-inch pipes** is considered roughly equivalent in suspended sprinkler weight, but only if the fixation transfers **vertical shear** and not bending. In the final email dated **2026-07-08**, Kebba replies that they can proceed with the proposed approach so they can use the **Exotec-provided brackets**.

Next steps implied in the thread are to **revise the proposal drawing** to match the required support height and attachment method, and to account for Exotec’s hole size/access limitations. The design appears to be moving toward approval, but only after the assembly details are aligned with Exotec’s mechanical requirements and the final configuration uses the provided brackets correctly.
```

```text
This document is an email thread about the **vertical pipe/sprinkler attachment design for the McLane Jazz Project**. It is a technical coordination discussion rather than a formal spec or contract. The purpose is to agree on how sprinkler support pipes and struts should be mounted to the rack uprights while staying compatible with Exotec’s rack design and robot operations.

The main issues discussed are **attachment method, bolt size, pipe sizing, placement height, and structural loading**. Kebba Mass proposes using the **holes on the flange of the uprights** to attach the strut and asks about bolt size, height, pipe dimensions, and added weight. Exotec’s Sebastien Cabot responds that the **upright holes are 9 mm**, so **1/2-inch bolts are not compatible**, and the **front upright holes are not reachable** because the robot arm passes close to them. He also states that the rack was designed for **2 vertical 3-inch pipes**, so **3 vertical 2-inch pipes** is acceptable from a weight standpoint, but the attachment must not create bending on the uprights—only **vertical shear**. He further notes that the vertical sprinkler support **041219-A** must be installed at the **same height as the horizontal beam** per the referenced rack detail, because placing it higher would introduce bending moment.

Several concrete project details are included. Kebba indicates the proposed strut would be mounted on the outside and not obstruct system operation, with **1/2-inch diameter bolts, 1.5 inches long**, at heights of **11'-0", 20'-6", and 30'-3" from the floor**. The vertical pipes are specified as **2-inch vertical tubes** compared with **1.5-inch horizontal pipes**, and the added load is stated as **154 pounds per vertical pipe including water**. Kebba also says the design has been reviewed with the designer and approved by the **JHA**, and mentions the need to confirm the extra weight with the **PE-stamped company**.

The latest outcome is a clear directive from Dadano Thouk: the **vertical sprinkler support 040045-A cannot be fixed to a front upright and must be fixed only to a rear upright**. This effectively resolves the attachment location question and indicates the team should proceed using the approved Exotec brackets and rear-upright mounting approach.
```

```text
This document is an email thread about a **technical coordination issue** for the **McLane Jazz Project**, specifically the **vertical pipe attachment / sprinkler support method**. It is not a contract or formal report, but an operational design discussion between **Alleato Group**, **Exotec**, and related project contacts. The purpose of the exchange is to confirm how sprinkler piping and strut supports should be mounted to the racking system without violating Exotec’s structural or access constraints.

The main topic is whether Alleato can attach struts and pipe clamps to the uprights using the available holes. **Kebba Mass** initially proposed using the flange holes on the uprights for the strut installation. **Sebastien Cabot** responded that there were two concerns: the upright holes are **9 mm**, so **1/2-inch bolts are not compatible**, and the **front upright holes are not accessible** because the robot arms pass too close to them. He also noted that the rack was designed to support **two vertical 3-inch pipes**, so **three vertical 2-inch pipes** would result in roughly equivalent suspended sprinkler weight and should be acceptable, as long as the load is transferred as **vertical shear only** and not as a bending force on the uprights.

The discussion then narrowed to the specific Exotec sprinkler bracket part **040045-A**. **Dadano Thouk** clarified that this vertical sprinkler support can be fixed **only to a rear upright**, not a front upright, and that it has a **specific profile/orientation**. Kebba accepted this constraint, stating the team would use that approach and avoid the front uprights so they could use the Exotec-provided brackets. Dadano then reconfirmed the correct orientation and showed that the previously indicated side would need to be on the opposite side.

The key action item is that the team should **install the vertical sprinkler support 040045-A only on rear uprights**, in the correct orientation, and avoid using the front uprights due to bolt compatibility and robot clearance issues. The final message from Kebba on **July 8, 2026 at 19:15 UTC** confirms agreement: “**Understood. Here is the correct orientation. Thank you!**”
```

📐 **City Stamp Box Requirement** (02:00 - 04:00)
City of Champaign requires a minimum 2 1/2 inch stamp box in the absolute top right corner of all drawings for approval.
No text should be included inside the stamp box to avoid delays.
✏️ **Drawing Updates and Title Blocks** (02:18 - 11:28)
Jerome will update drawings and send revised title blocks to consultants.
Dan will revise company templates to incorporate the new stamp box.
Updated plans will be sent back to Douglas for submission.
📅 **Meeting Schedule and Project Timeline** (07:07 - 08:49)
Project start date targeted for August 1 or August 3.
Weekly meetings to scale back to biweekly after July 30th following July 16th meeting.
Meetings may reduce further to every three weeks or monthly during construction phase.
👷 **Construction Phase Coordination** (05:50 - 08:49)
Nick assigned as superintendent and onsite contact moving forward.
Team agreed to maintain communication despite reduced meeting frequency.
Coordination and issue resolution progressing well.
📄 **Professional Design Registration** (09:23 - 10:00)
Registration process ongoing with paper application mailed.
Uncertainty about processing time with the state of Illinois.
📞 **Communication and Follow-up** (06:50 - 10:55)
Douglas encouraged team to reach out if issues arise.
Continuous communication emphasized during submittal and construction phases.

## Notes
## **Drawing Approval and Compliance**

The team agreed to adjust the drawing layout to meet the city of Champaign’s strict requirement for the stamp box placement.

- **City mandates stamp box in top right corner of every drawing sheet** to comply with review standards (01:24)  
    - Denise and the city team insisted on this exact location without exception  
    - Jerome will update the drawings and distribute a new title block to all consultants immediately  
    - Douglas will collect updated PDFs and upload them to Denise to restart the review process  
    - This ensures the submission meets city approval requirements and avoids further delays  

- **Clarification on stamp box text removed to meet city standards** (04:05)  
    - The box must be at least **2.5 inches** with no additional text inside, only reserved space for the city review stamp  
    - Dan flagged possible wording issues that might cause resubmission; Douglas confirmed no text is needed  
    - This reduces risk of rejection due to incorrect labeling on the drawings  

## **Meeting Cadence and Project Oversight Transition**

The weekly meeting schedule will scale back after key upcoming reviews to align with project phases and resource needs.

- **Plan to continue weekly meetings through July 23rd, then switch to bi-weekly starting July 30th** to match project progress (07:07)  
    - La’Kisha confirmed the upcoming project start around August 1st or 3rd, possibly triggering issues to discuss  
    - Jerome and Douglas agreed bi-weekly meetings will suffice once the first round of submittals concludes  
    - Continuous, informal communication will remain for urgent construction needs outside scheduled meetings  
    - This reduces meeting overhead while maintaining timely issue resolution during critical phases  

- **Nick designated as on-site superintendent and main contact for field coordination** (05:54)  
    - The team will shift operational oversight to Nick as construction begins  
    - This defines clear accountability for on-the-ground issues and keeps project communication efficient  

## **Regulatory Submittal Process and Timing**

The team reviewed current status and expectations for professional design registration and city approvals.

- **Waiting on professional design firm registration submission for CR number validation** as key outstanding step (09:23)  
    - Jerome confirmed the application was mailed with online payment completed but no online submission option existed  
    - Douglas and Dan discussed typical processing times, noting it is a one-time process that likely takes weeks but varies by jurisdiction  
    - This step is a gating factor for full city approval and project progression  

- **City of Champaign requires professional registration linked to CR numbers for final approval** (10:00)  
    - The team awaits confirmation that this registration is processed before proceeding with further submittals  
    - Ongoing monitoring of this process is critical to avoid delays in starting construction  

## **Document Control and Coordination**

Clear responsibility was assigned to update and distribute the correct title blocks across all project documents.

- **Jerome will update and email the revised title block to all consultants today** to maintain consistency (11:06)  
    - Dan will adjust his company title block accordingly and return changes promptly  
    - This ensures all parties use the same updated document format to avoid confusion in submissions  
    - Timely distribution supports smooth coordination and reduces back-and-forth on formatting issues  

- **Finalized drawings will be uploaded and shared once all updates are complete** (02:18)  
    - Douglas will manage uploading the corrected plans to Denise for city review  
    - This centralizes document control and helps track submission status  

## **Project Progress and Team Feedback**

The team acknowledged positive momentum in the project, with only a few outstanding items pending.

- **La’Kisha praised progress and effective handling of current deliverables** (08:49)  
    - She confirmed no additional concerns at this time, indicating smooth workflow  
    - Douglas and Jerome echoed that outstanding issues are mostly external, related to professional registration and city reviews  
    - This positive tone supports confidence in meeting upcoming project milestones  

- **Jerome emphasized ongoing responsiveness despite fewer formal meetings** (06:50)  
    - He assured continuous contact for urgent needs even as meeting frequency decreases  
    - This approach balances efficiency with readiness for quick issue resolution  

## **Technology and Communication Tools**

There was a brief discussion clarifying automated meeting report notifications to avoid confusion.

- **Dan raised concerns about AI-generated meeting recap messages from “weekly Ace Hardware Champaign meeting” system** (04:39)  
    - Douglas clarified these are automated recaps with no required action, and can be ignored or deleted  
    - Nick confirmed this system is intended to help track meeting topics but is not interactive  
    - This understanding prevents wasted time and confusion over automated messages during project coordination

## Summary
- **Solar System Design:** Roof engineered to support up to 250 kW solar load, avoiding costly redesigns if capacity increases.
- **Engineering Reviews:** Electrical and structural engineers to review specs beforehand to ensure efficient, focused technical meetings.
- **Installation Risk:** BIPV system installs like metal roofing, minimizing risks of wind damage, roof penetrations, and delays.
- **Project Scheduling:** Coordination planned for design review meeting; roof layout accuracy key for structural calculations.
- **Utility Coordination:** Site visits needed to verify private water/sewer connections; documents must be submitted as a complete package.
- **Product Management:** Volleyball light samples shared for review; emphasis on educating team about new solar system to ease adoption.

## Short Summary
The team discussed advancing the solar project with a temporary 45-kilowatt system, designed to support future upgrades to 250 kilowatts without structural redesign. Structural engineers will design a steel truss roof to accommodate the higher load, reducing potential costs and delays. Electrical and structural engineers will review installation specifications prior to meetings to ensure focused discussions. The BIPV system installation was noted to have lower risks compared to traditional solar systems, avoiding issues like wind damage. Team coordination was prioritized, scheduling a design review meeting contingent on document prep. Utility verification was also discussed, requiring site visits for proper connections, while volleyball light samples were shared for design review.

## Gist
The meeting focused on planning a solar project, emphasizing system design and team coordination to ensure efficiency and avoid delays.

## Bullet Gist
☀️ Solar System Design: Roof engineered to support up to 250 kW solar load, avoiding costly redesigns if capacity increases.
🔍 Engineering Reviews: Electrical and structural engineers to review specs beforehand to ensure efficient, focused technical meetings.
⚙️ Installation Risk: BIPV system installs like metal roofing, minimizing risks of wind damage, roof penetrations, and delays.
📅 Project Scheduling: Coordination planned for design review meeting; roof layout accuracy key for structural calculations.
🔌 Utility Coordination: Site visits needed to verify private water/sewer connections; documents must be submitted as a complete package.
💡 Product Management: Volleyball light samples shared for review; emphasis on educating team about new solar system to ease adoption.

## Shorthand Bullet
☀️ **Solar System Decision and Design** (01:08 - 05:18)
Decision made to proceed with a 45 kW solar system as a placeholder to avoid delays.
Discussion about potential increase to 250 kW and preference to design roof structures accordingly to prevent rework.
Proposal to switch from wood to metal trusses for stronger roof support with minimal impact on solar panel spacing or weight.
Integration of standing seam metal roofing and building load assessments considered.
📑 **Preparation and Document Distribution** (05:18 - 08:03)
Suggestion for all engineers to review installation guides and product specs before design meetings to focus discussions on specific questions.
Confirmation that key installation guides and spec sheets have been shared with essential parties.
Clarification needed on whether Brian Birch received documents for bidding purposes.
🔆 **BIPV System Advantages and Coordination** (08:03 - 09:23)
BIPV solar system installation is less complicated than traditional arrays, requiring no roof penetrations.
Weight of BIPV system comparable to standing seam metal roofing, alleviating concerns about load.
Plan to hold meetings with electrical and structural engineers only after preparatory review; follow-up calls scheduled if needed.
🏐 **Volleyball Lighting and Design Updates** (10:13 - 12:30)
Volleyball light specifications received and sent to design team for review.
Additional requests for color and texture samples shared with offers to send more based on preferences.
Revised Boone County design release sent after architect review due to prior survey data issues.
Clarified preference for package submission to city planning to avoid delays.
🚰 **Utility Coordination and Meeting Wrap-Up** (12:30 - 15:10)
Discussion about locating exact private water and sewer connections with willingness from site contact to show locations physically or via drawings.
Urgency noted due to upcoming travel on the 16th.
Agreement to coordinate document reviews and follow-up scheduling as necessary.
Participant offers availability for support calls when needed.

## Summary

- **Quality Control:** Early issue detection and escalation prevent costly fixes; proactive communication and internal deadlines are vital.

## Short Summary
The discussion centered on multiple key aspects of team performance, including compensation and employee recognition, where Mike Parsons appreciated his unexpected raise, reinforcing the belief in his potential. Quality control was highlighted with lessons learned from the Uniqlo project, stressing the importance of proactive issue identification and escalation. The need for operational excellence was emphasized through hands-on involvement and the development of SOPs, which contribute to efficiency. Training needs for technical skills, specifically in areas like 3D modeling and AutoCAD, were identified for improvement. Clear communication and accountability were recognized as vital for team trust and leadership readiness. Lastly, discussions on the Bluebeam Max trial and improving access to training materials were also noted as essential for consistent project standards.

## Gist
The meeting focused on employee recognition, quality control, training needs, and communication to enhance project management and operational efficiency.

## Notes
## **Compensation and Recognition**

The team recognizes and values employee contributions through targeted raises and clear acknowledgment of effort.

- **Acknowledgment of compensation satisfaction and potential** was confirmed by Mike Parsons, who appreciated the unexpected raise at the year’s start as a sign of recognition, with Brandon Clymer and Jesse Dawson reinforcing their confidence in Mike’s potential and ongoing contribution (01:12).
    - Jesse highlighted that consistent internal motivation and proactive effort are key for growth and promotion.
    - Brandon and Jesse emphasized that being recognized financially aligns with the belief in Mike’s ability to advance within the company.
    - The positive compensation environment is intended to maintain motivation and reinforce performance standards.
    - Recognition supports retention and aligns individual goals with company growth ambitions.

## **Quality Control and Issue Management**

Proactive issue identification and escalation are critical to maintaining project quality and avoiding costly remediation.

- **Reflection on a costly oversight at the Uniqlo project** regarding oxidation on vertical piping revealed a failure to catch and fix issues proactively, leading to unexpected remedial costs and client concerns (02:27).
    - Mike acknowledged this as a valuable lesson in the importance of staying ahead of problems rather than reacting late.
    - Brandon stressed the need to escalate issues promptly with potential solutions rather than waiting for management intervention.
    - Jesse highlighted that maintaining a clean, organized site and controlling known variables can mitigate many issues.
    - The team agreed that early communication and defined internal deadlines for problem resolution are essential to avoid job delays.

- **Commitment to continuous improvement in problem-solving and leadership** was expressed, including pushing technical skills and increasing proactive communication with other superintendents to catch problems early (19:55).
    - Mike plans to improve drawing markups and 3D project representations for better transparency.
    - Brandon suggested iterative approaches such as creating weekly project update videos as a practical alternative to complex 3D modeling.
    - Kebba Mass confirmed that a communication system is being implemented to enhance information flow among superintendents.

## **Operational Excellence and Process Development**

Hands-on involvement combined with developing procedural standards drives efficiency and long-term success.

- **Mike’s contributions beyond his role** include developing SOPs and assisting field crews during critical project phases, which has improved efficiency and reduced errors (11:04).
    - Brandon and Jesse praised Mike’s willingness to physically engage in challenging tasks, though they noted that proactive planning should reduce the need for such interventions.
    - Kebba added that this engagement helps Mike understand the work deeply, enhancing SOP quality.
    - Brandon encouraged leveraging client and integrator relationships during site visits to generate sales and business growth opportunities.
    - Kebba noted the importance of gathering project intelligence from subcontractors to identify potential new work.

- **Emphasis on training and knowledge sharing** is seen as essential to creating a replicable system that allows others to manage jobs effectively (50:50).
    - Brandon envisions Mike evolving into a field operations director overseeing multiple projects.
    - Jesse underlined the importance of Mike setting standards that new superintendents can follow to maintain consistent quality across projects.
    - The team plans to leverage Mike’s site expertise to train others and maintain project standards remotely through regular check-ins and digital communication.

## **Training and Skill Development**

Expanding technical skills through targeted training and self-directed learning enhances both individual and company capabilities.

- **Training needs focus on 3D modeling, advanced drawing markup, AutoCAD, racking systems, and hydraulic calculations** to improve technical proficiency and project management quality (45:01).
    - Mike expressed uncertainty about specific courses and welcomed guidance on available options.
    - Brandon stressed the importance of proactive research by Mike to identify and propose relevant training programs.
    - Brandon recommended self-assessment to prioritize skills and suggested practical learning approaches like peer mentoring and focused YouTube tutorials.
    - Kebba confirmed SketchUp as an accessible tool that can be learned quickly and efficiently for project visualization.
    - The leadership team supports providing financial resources for approved training but expects initiative in selecting appropriate programs.

- **Balancing time investment between learning new skills and jobsite responsibilities** is a priority (25:30).
    - Brandon and Jesse advised that jobsite management and schedule adherence take precedence during work hours.
    - Learning complex software should be pursued during personal time or in manageable increments to avoid impacting operational duties.
    - Alternative communication tools, such as short progress videos, can deliver client transparency without heavy technical overhead.

## **Communication and Leadership Style**

Clear, timely communication and accountability foster trust and effective team management.

- **Mike’s communication skills are a strong asset**, ensuring clear, professional interaction with clients, contractors, and teams, which builds trust and helps resolve issues independently (17:07).
    - Jesse emphasized that developing the ability to handle problems without frequent escalation is a sign of a seasoned superintendent.
    - Brandon encouraged bringing well-thought-out problem analyses and potential solutions to management to accelerate resolution and learning.
    - Kebba highlighted the need for urgency in treating all problems as critical, promoting early discussion and collaborative resolution.
    - The team agreed that establishing internal deadlines for resolving issues helps keep projects on track and ensures accountability.

- **Managerial accountability and trust** were recognized as key drivers motivating Mike to work confidently and independently (31:52).
    - Mike appreciates the support and availability of Brandon, Jesse, and Kebba, which enables him to represent the company effectively.
    - Brandon and Jesse highlighted that as the sprinkler division grows, Mike’s role will evolve toward leadership with broader responsibility.
    - The team acknowledged the competitive market pressures, noting that excellent project management is essential to retain clients and prevent loss to lower-cost competitors.

## **Tools and Systems Implementation**

Adoption of new tools and improved access to training materials are ongoing efforts to support operational efficiency.

- **Bluebeam Max trial and selective rollout** are underway, with limited seats allocated based on usage and need, not full company deployment (52:48).
    - Kebba was informed to coordinate with Maria to receive access during the trial phase.
    - Brandon clarified the intent to evaluate the tool’s value before wider purchase decisions.

- **Awareness and accessibility of company training videos and SOPs need improvement** (38:00).
    - Mike was unaware of the location of company-wide training videos despite using division-specific SOP folders.
    - Brandon and Jesse acknowledged the gap in communication about resource locations and plan to fix this.
    - The team sees making these materials more visible as critical for ongoing training and performance consistency.

## Keywords
- Compensation
- Leadership
- Training
- Communication
- Project Management
- SOP Development

- Continue proactive improvement in field leadership and problem escalation, ensuring timely communication and collaborative problem solving (07:00)
- Increase communication and regular check-ins with other sprinkler project superintendents to improve coordination and project updates (20:48)

- Arrange and join the call with Kebba Mass and Mike Parsons to address Uniqlo California project scope questions (53:50)

  {
    "index": 452,
    "speaker": "Jesse Dawson",
    "text": "I mean it'd be great, you know, as we expand or we hire a new superintendent, then we send them out to Mike, they spend a week or two or a month and when they show up on your job site, they know exact",

  },
  {

    "speaker": "Brandon Clymer",
    "text": "And the more that you can take over, you know, the field stuff and to where you're running these issues and you're getting stuff done to where I can spend more time with Keva refining how estimates go",

  },

  },

```text
This document is an email thread about a **contract scope negotiation** for a construction project, specifically focused on **site work and concrete** responsibilities. The correspondence is between **Tony Courtney (Project Manager)** and **Jesse Dawson (COO)** at Alleato Group, with input from **Merritt Contracting** and references to another subcontractor, **Precision**. The purpose of the thread is to resolve contract revisions, confirm who will perform which parts of the job, and determine whether Merritt will still handle the work under the revised terms.

A major topic is the **division of scope** between earthwork/site work and concrete. Merritt proposed several contract changes, including revised language around **anchor bolts** (“we do not provide”), removal of **joint sealant** from item #23, and elimination of **wheel stops / parking lot accessories / anchorage** from the civil scope. Tony initially pushed for a quick signature and asked for the earliest possible start date for demo, erosion control, and construction drive work. Jesse asked for the next lowest bids for concrete and site work, and emphasized that the start could not wait until October, advising Tony to speak with the right person at Merritt to understand why so many changes were being requested.

The key scheduling and cost details are that Merritt’s start time was being discussed as **October** at one point, which Jesse said would not work. Tony then found backup options: first, **Precision** indicated they could send a crew **on or before August 1** to handle demo, gravel drive, erosion control, and related site work; later Tony reported another earthwork company that could also start **on or before August 1** and was **$51,000 less expensive**, which would help offset project overages already incurred. The thread suggests that Alleato was trying to reduce cost and avoid schedule delays by splitting the work if Merritt would only continue with the concrete portion.

Next steps were to **get the revised contract signed**, confirm whether Merritt would remain on just the concrete scope, and possibly **shift the earthwork/site work to the lower-cost alternative**. Jesse’s direction was not to “fire Merritt,” but to contact the proper person and clarify the contract changes. ### DOCUMENTS | PRINTING CHECKS SOP.pdf
```

Source ID: S82 = sharepoint_01AFX6IXKYOPHGF5GMSBFJHDN4JQJHQ327
Project: Unassigned
Source at: 2026-07-09T16:23:49.444Z
URL: https://alleato.sharepoint.com/sites/AlleatoGroup/Shared%20Documents/Alleato%20Group/Alleato%20Group-Shared/SOP/HR/PRINTING%20CHECKS%20SOP.pdf

```text
This document is a standard operating procedure (SOP) for printing, preparing, and mailing checks that accounting sends out weekly by email. Its main purpose is to ensure checks are printed correctly, tracked in the organization’s system, signed, assembled, and mailed without errors. It is an internal process guide rather than a contract or report.

The SOP walks through the full workflow in sequence: open the emailed checks attachment, confirm the printer is connected and set to the correct paper settings, and use the system print dialog with the correct check-paper option selected. It emphasizes that the blue setting indicates check paper is being used and that the check paper must be loaded in the printer facing the correct direction (upside down and opposite the user). After printing, the process includes cutting off the remittance slip, organizing slips numerically by check number, and making sure the slips match the last check slip.

The document also requires logging each check in Notion under the “Printing Check(s) Log” with specific information: check number, location of the printed check, who printed it, mail-out date, check status, the batch link from SharePoint or the email, and who signed it. Another key detail is that if a check covers more than 10 invoices, the system creates a second “Voided Check” for remaining invoices, and the bottom stub from that voided check must also be included in communications. The final steps are to have Brandon sign the checks, obtain Alleato envelopes with the clear front window, fold and seal the checks, stamp them, and mail them out as soon as possible.
```

### DOCUMENTS | Alleato_Candidate_FAQ_Knowledge_Sheet.docx

Source ID: S83 = sharepoint_01AFX6IXO7IH44U7ZRQRGJI5FPD7P3X77R
Project: Unassigned
Source at: 2026-07-09T16:23:51.964Z
URL: https://alleato.sharepoint.com/sites/AlleatoGroup/_layouts/15/Doc.aspx?sourcedoc=%7BCAF941DF-317F-4C84-9474-AF1FDFBBFFF1%7D&file=Alleato_Candidate_FAQ_Knowledge_Sheet.docx&action=default&mobileredirect=true

```text
This document is an internal candidate FAQ knowledge sheet, likely used by recruiters or hiring managers at Alleato to answer applicant questions consistently. It is not a contract or policy manual, but a reference guide summarizing current compensation, benefits, PTO, remote-work expectations, review cadence, company growth, and technology use. The content is organized as a Q&A by topic, with a brief source note at the end indicating the information was gathered from interviews in late March and early April 2026.

The compensation and benefits section explains that Alleato hires both W-2 employees and 1099 contractors, depending on role. W-2 office and field roles such as PMs, PEs, superintendents, and office managers receive full benefits, while some accounting/bookkeeping roles have been hired as remote 1099 contractors with flat hourly pay and no health insurance, PTO accrual, or 401(k) match. For full-time roles, the sheet cites a health plan that begins after the first full month of employment, company-paid single coverage worth about $8,500 per year, an employee contribution for family coverage of roughly $400–500 per month, a 401(k) with a 4% match, annual bonuses up to 20% of salary, a 10% fee referral/business-development incentive on jobs brought in personally, and car/phone allowances that vary by role. Signing bonuses and relocation help are not standard but may be offered case by case.

The PTO and work-flexibility sections note that standard PTO for a recent project engineer offer was two weeks per year, plus federally recognized paid holidays, while more senior or hard-to-fill roles may negotiate more—one example cited was four weeks of PTO plus a phone allowance. PTO requests must be submitted by the employee through the Venture/TimeCo system, and maternity/paternity leave is covered by a written company policy distributed by HR (Ashley Ortiz), with one example of two weeks of paternity leave and temporary work-from-home flexibility. The document also says unused PTO is handled at termination by HR/payroll according to state law, not by managers.

Other sections describe Alleato as a fast-growing company founded about 3–4 years ago, expanding from roughly 3 employees to 30+ in about two years and hiring across Indianapolis, St. Petersburg/Tampa, and other markets. The company claims to use modern tools and AI actively, including ChatGPT/Claude, Procore, Bluebeam, Job Planner, and Acumatica. The main next step implied throughout the sheet is to confirm role-specific details with HR before promising anything to candidates, since benefits, PTO, remote status, allowances, and travel expectations vary by req and by seniority.
```

```text
This document is an email chain about setting up a **joint check agreement** for the **EXOL project in Morrisville, Pennsylvania**. It is not a contract itself, but a coordination thread between Alleato, Wargo Interior Systems, and Kamco Bldg Supply to establish how payments for drywall/material invoices will be handled. The main purpose is to confirm the right contacts, explain the arrangement, and get the agreement reviewed and signed.

The key topic is that **Wargo Interiors has exceeded its credit limit with its supplier, Kamco**, because of the amount of work on the job, so the parties want to use a **joint check setup**. John Merulla from Kamco says Alleato and Wargo will both receive emails for the job, and that **Alleato will cut material checks jointly to Kamco and Wargo**. Craig Cassel from Wargo provides Bob Wright’s contact information as the Alleato contact handling transactions, and Bob then forwards the issue to Misty Rogers asking her to review it, noting he has **not done a joint check agreement before** and that the project is **scheduled to start next week**.

Important details include dates and contact information: Craig’s email is dated **July 6, 2026**, John’s follow-up is also **July 6, 2026**, Bob forwards the request on **July 8, 2026**, and Misty replies on **July 9, 2026**. John asks Bob to provide an email address for receipt of invoices and instructs him to **review the attached paperwork, sign if he agrees, and return it**; once Kamco receives it, they will forward it to Wargo for their review and signature, then send a completed copy to all parties. A side note from Kamco states that **as of May 1, 2026, a 2% processing fee applies to all credit card payments to AR accounts**.

Next steps are clear: **Bob/Alleato should review the joint check paperwork, determine whether to agree, sign it if acceptable, and send it back to John Merulla at Kamco**. After that, Kamco will route it to Wargo for signature and distribute the final executed copy. Misty’s final note indicates a possible issue with the forwarded email thread: she says she **thought the attachment would forward too**, implying the attachment may still need to be resent or located before the review can proceed.
```

## Summary
- **Client Communication:** Improve question clarity with detailed, marked-up drawings; proactively lead questions to avoid confusion and frustration.

- **Power Routing Clarification:** Confirmed charging station power to run via overhead drops, not underground, to prevent misinformation and unnecessary concerns.

- **Schedule Management:** Break down warehouse TI and office schedules into detailed sub-activities; include permit milestones to track delays impacting January completion.

- **Submittal and Procurement Logs:** Current logs are incomplete for $25M project; require expansion, proper tracking, and alignment with schedule to avoid delays.

- **Team Coordination:** Ensure internal alignment before contacting client; increase collaboration on schedule updates to deliver credible timelines.

- **Key Risks:** Permit approval delays and poor log accuracy threaten schedule; consistent communication and realistic schedules with buffers critical to maintain trust.

## Short Summary
The meeting focused on improving client communication through clearer and more detailed questions, emphasizing proactive strategies to avoid confusion and frustration. Key clarifications included the routing of charging station power, which was confirmed to be overhead, avoiding costs related to underground installations. The need for a more detailed project schedule was highlighted, requiring breakdowns of sub-activities to manage timelines effectively. Challenges with incomplete submittal and procurement logs were acknowledged, necessitating immediate action to prevent delays. Internal alignment and coordination among the team were stressed to maintain credibility and client relations, considering the high stakes of the project for future opportunities.

## Gist
The meeting aimed to address project communication and scheduling to enhance client trust and align expectations.

## Bullet Gist
🗣️ Client Communication: Improve question clarity with detailed, marked-up drawings; proactively lead questions to avoid confusion and frustration.
⚡ Power Routing Clarification: Confirmed charging station power to run via overhead drops, not underground, to prevent misinformation and unnecessary concerns.
📆 Schedule Management: Break down warehouse TI and office schedules into detailed sub-activities; include permit milestones to track delays impacting January completion.
📋 Submittal and Procurement Logs: Current logs are incomplete for $25M project; require expansion, proper tracking, and alignment with schedule to avoid delays.
🤝 Team Coordination: Ensure internal alignment before contacting client; increase collaboration on schedule updates to deliver credible timelines.

## Shorthand Bullet
📝 **Improving Project Questions and Client Communication** (00:00 - 07:41)
Emphasis on improving quality and detail of project questions with organized, marked-up inquiries referencing specific drawings.
Correction of misinformation regarding electrical conduit for charging stations; agreement on overhead power drops instead of underground conduit.
Acknowledgement of client Steve’s feedback on team alignment and communication, focusing on over-delivering for future projects.
📋 **Use of Tools and Schedule Management** (07:41 - 13:48)
Use of Job Planner and RFI tracking to manage open questions, submittals, and construction issues in a detailed manner.
Need for a reliable, actively tracked project schedule to provide transparency and manage client expectations.
Permit submittal timing identified as a key scheduling dependency.
🏗️ **Current Project Status and Upcoming Work** (14:00 - 20:52)
Discussion of door removal, concrete pouring, CMU wall work, and electrical layout steps for the upcoming week.
Warehouse Tenant Improvement (TI) schedule planned to start August 21, covering main office, trucker’s lounge, and restrooms with about 98 days duration.
Identification of incomplete schedule activities related to steel reinforcement, trusses, curbs, and mechanical equipment installation needing detailed breakdown.
📅 **Schedule Revision and Documentation** (19:16 - 26:05)
Commitment to revise and expand the schedule with realistic durations and logic to avoid optimistic completion dates.
Submittal and procurement logs currently insufficient and require urgent updating and streamlining.
Coordination among team members to clean up project logs, finalize schedule, and ensure documentation meets client standards.

## Notes
## **Client Communication and Quality of Questions**

The team must improve the clarity and detail of client questions to enhance trust and project alignment.

- **Client feedback demands urgent and detailed questions with marked-up drawings** to avoid confusion and reduce frustration, as emphasized by Brandon referencing Steve's concerns (00:01)
    - Steve highlighted that questions often arrive either too late or lack proper detail, with poor referencing of drawings
    - The team must lead the questioning process proactively rather than being reactive or unclear
    - Detailed questions should focus on true design changes or clarifications, avoiding trivial or vague inquiries
    - Using tools like Job Planner to track questions and mark drawings will help simplify communication for the client

- **Clarification on charging station power routing resolved to overhead drops, not underground**, reaffirmed by Bob and Brandon to prevent misinformation (02:39)
    - Initial confusion arose when Daniel allegedly told Steve that Bob recommended underground conduit
    - Bob confirmed the design call concluded unanimously to run power overhead with drops, avoiding slab trenching and costs
    - Brandon stressed the importance of avoiding unnecessary questions that imply major costly changes without justification
    - This episode illustrates the need for clear internal communication before involving the client to maintain credibility

- **The team commits to organized tracking of open items and questions**, with Jesse tasked to collaborate on maintaining an updated log in Job Planner or Excel (10:50)
    - Jesse and Brandon both have experience working with Steve and understand the importance of clear, direct communication
    - The team will track questions with room and drawing references to improve precision
    - This system will help ensure all parties stay informed and reduce delays caused by unclear or late inquiries

## **Project Scheduling and Timeline Management**

A detailed, realistic, and approved schedule is critical to meet contract deadlines and maintain client confidence.

- **Current schedule lacks clarity and needs breaking down to realistic sub-activities**, especially for warehouse TI and office areas, to avoid underestimating durations (15:12)
    - Parker has a base schedule starting warehouse TI on August 21 with a total duration of **98 days** including steel reinforcement, mechanical, and electrical work
    - Jesse highlighted the need to divide large activities into smaller tasks such as roof truss setting, curb installation, and unit setting for better tracking
    - Brandon emphasized the importance of accurate logic linking activities to allow schedule flexibility and avoid overly optimistic finish dates

- **Permit submittal remains the key gating item delaying schedule finalization**, with Exile blocking submission through ongoing design changes (12:49)
    - Permit submission target is currently uncertain; if submitted by August 17, a five-week review would push approval to August 23
    - Jesse suggested embedding permit submission and approval dates as driving milestones in the schedule to clearly show impacts of delays
    - Without permit approval, many schedule activities cannot be finalized, risking extension beyond planned January completion

- **Deadline to finalize and submit an accurate schedule is set for Wednesday to support contract signing next week**, as stated by Brandon (19:31)
    - Parker committed to updating the schedule with more detailed activities and durations tonight and tomorrow morning
    - Jesse offered to assist with reviewing drawings and plugging in realistic durations for all trades
    - Brandon expects the project timeline to realistically extend into March next year despite hopes for January finish

## **Submittal and Procurement Tracking**

The submittal and procurement logs are incomplete and inaccurate, requiring immediate overhaul to support schedule and procurement planning.

- **Current submittal log is insufficient for a $25 million project with only 14 items logged**, per Brandon’s review (23:42)
    - Bob acknowledged the log needs streamlining and expansion to remove irrelevant entries and add missing items
    - Brandon stressed the need for a proper numbering and tracking system with workflow logic to manage approvals and deadlines
    - Jesse questioned whether Demetrius has fully built out the submittal log, revealing partial progress with ongoing efforts

- **Procurement log must align with the schedule to prevent delays in material and equipment delivery**, as emphasized by Brandon (24:52)
    - Key long-lead items from RJ Skeleton and other suppliers require input to schedule critical installation activities
    - Coordination between Demetrius, Bob, and Parker is necessary to ensure procurement dates feed directly into schedule logic
    - The lack of accurate submittal and procurement data currently risks misalignment and schedule slippage

- **Ownership and accountability for submittal and procurement tracking identified**, with Demetrius and Bob responsible for managing and cleaning up logs (23:10)
    - Brandon offered support and guidance if the team needs help establishing proper processes
    - This cleanup is critical for enabling Parker to confidently update and finalize the schedule with accurate lead times

## **Team Coordination and Responsiveness**

Effective collaboration and clear communication protocols are essential to avoid misunderstandings and improve project delivery.

- **Brandon stressed the importance of internal alignment before escalating to the client**, especially regarding design decisions and communications (10:50)
    - Example: Daniel calling Steve with inaccurate info on underground conduit caused unnecessary confusion
    - Brandon suggested a rule that team members call him before escalating issues to Steve to maintain message consistency
    - This approach is intended to build trust and avoid client frustration that could jeopardize future work

- **Bob, Jesse, and Parker agreed to increase coordination**, with Jesse offering availability to review schedules and drawings with Parker promptly (25:45)
    - Parker will work on schedule updates overnight and into the next day, with Jesse’s support to refine task breakdowns and durations
    - This collaboration aims to produce a credible schedule for contractual commitments and client review

- **The team recognizes the critical nature of this client relationship**, aiming to position themselves for future projects involving 35 buildings over three years (06:28)
    - Brandon highlighted the high stakes, with each building worth between **$10 million and $30 million**
    - Over-delivering on this project is strategic to securing a large share of upcoming work, reinforcing the need for quality communication and scheduling discipline

## **Key Risks and Mitigation Plans**

Several risks threaten project delivery, but the team has identified approaches to manage and mitigate these challenges.

- **Permit approval delays remain the biggest risk to schedule stability and project start dates** (12:49)
    - Permit submission is currently blocked by ongoing design changes from Exile
    - Clear communication of permit status and its impact on the schedule to all stakeholders is necessary to manage expectations
    - Including permit submission and review milestones in the schedule will help highlight delay impacts

- **Inaccurate or incomplete submittal and procurement logs risk schedule slippage and poor resource coordination** (23:42)
    - The team must clean and expand these logs immediately to ensure all lead times are captured
    - Brandon’s offer to assist with workflow setup will help improve process rigor and visibility

- **Poor communication or misrepresentations to the client could damage trust and future business** (01:35)
    - The team must provide consistent, clear messaging internally before client contact
    - Detailed, documented questions tracked through proper tools will reduce misunderstandings
    - Brandon’s experience with Steve guides the team to anticipate sensitivities and align communications accordingly

- **Schedule optimism creates risk of missed deadlines and client dissatisfaction** (21:17)
    - The team agreed to build realistic durations and logic with buffers
    - Jesse and Parker will work together to ensure the schedule reflects achievable timelines
    - Brandon stressed that underpromising and overdelivering is preferable to avoid reputation damage

## Keywords
- Project Scheduling
- Electrical Design
- Client Communication
- Submittal Log
- Construction Permit
- Warehouse Tenant Improvement

## Action Items
**Bob Wright**
- Reformat and include detailed room and drawing references in question documents to meet client expectations (08:13)
- Streamline and complete the submittal log, removing unnecessary items (23:32)
- Continue efforts with Demetrius and others to ensure procurement and submittals are on track and properly logged (23:10)
**Parker Hollingsworth**
- Share the current project schedule and update it to reflect permit submission dependencies and new dates (12:07)
- Add detailed activities to the schedule, especially for warehouse TI, steel reinforcement, and mechanical installations, with realistic durations and proper logic sequences (16:10)
- Complete an updated, accurate schedule by Wednesday next week to align with contract finalization (19:37)
- Work with Jesse Dawson to review and refine the schedule with subarea detail and appropriate time buffers (18:50)
- Edit schedule to reflect permit submission lead times and share with client to communicate impact of delays (13:15)
- Delete or void draft RFIs that are outdated or resolved to maintain cleanliness in tracking (22:17)
- Collaborate with Bob Wright and team to keep procurement and submittal logs accurate and integrated into scheduling (24:30)
- Coordinate on site for electrical layout and delivery planning (14:40)
**Jesse Dawson**
- Assist Parker Hollingsworth in reviewing and building out the detailed schedule and logic (20:14)
- Support organizing detailed tracking logs and ensuring tools like Job Planner are effectively utilized (11:44)
- Participate in schedule review meetings as needed to ensure accuracy and client readiness (25:45)
**Brandon Clymer**
- Lead efforts to improve quality and timeliness of client communications and questions (00:01)
- Follow up with Dan about communication protocols to avoid client misinforming (10:50)
- Oversee development and review of schedule and contract to ensure alignment with project realities (19:40)
- Drive collaboration among the team to maintain detailed tracking of open items, RFIs, submittals, and procurement (25:24)

    "text": "Let me finish the comment before you jump in.",

  },
  {

    "speaker": "Brandon Clymer",
    "text": "Okay?",
    "filters": {

      "text_cleanup": "Okay?",
      "sentiment": "neutral"

  },
  {
    "index": 64,
    "speaker": "Brandon Clymer",
    "text": "Can you, can we agree to that?",
    "filters": {

      "text_cleanup": "Can you, can we agree to that?",
      "sentiment": "positive"
    }
  },
  {
    "index": 68,
    "speaker": "Brandon Clymer",
    "text": "Now what I'm asking is like, is where was the idea or something came up from as far as running them underground?",
    "filters": {
      "question": "Now what I'm asking is like, is where was the idea or something came up from as far as running them underground?",
      "text_cleanup": "Now what I'm asking is like, is where was the idea or something came up from as far as running them underground?",

    }
  },
  {
    "index": 69,
    "speaker": "Brandon Clymer",
    "text": "Somebody had to come up with it, right?",
    "filters": {
      "question": "Somebody had to come up with it, right?",
      "text_cleanup": "Somebody had to come up with it, right?",
      "sentiment": "neutral"
    }
  },
  {
    "index": 73,
    "speaker": "Bob  Wright",
    "text": "I said, well, you know, let's, let's take a look at it.",
    "filters": {
      "task": "I said, well, you know, let's, let's take a look at it.",
      "text_cleanup": "I said, well, you know, let's, let's take a look at it.",
      "sentiment": "neutral"
    }
  },
  {
    "index": 78,
    "speaker": "Brandon Clymer",
    "text": "So you're saying that in the design call with, with RJ and all of our designers, there was a conversation like, do we run these underground or overhead?",
    "filters": {
      "question": "So you're saying that in the design call with, with RJ and all of our designers, there was a conversation like, do we run these underground or overhead?",
      "text_cleanup": "So you're saying that in the design call with, with RJ and all of our designers, there was a conversation like, do we run these underground or overhead?",

  },
  {

    "speaker": "Bob  Wright",
    "text": "Make sure they don't want it underground.",
    "filters": {

      "text_cleanup": "Make sure they don't want it underground.",
      "sentiment": "negative"
    }
  },
  {
    "index": 94,
    "speaker": "Brandon Clymer",
    "text": "All I can tell you is, you know, talking with the client.",
    "filters": {
      "task": "All I can tell you is, you know, talking with the client.",
      "text_cleanup": "All I can tell you is, you know, talking with the client.",
      "sentiment": "neutral"
    }
  },
  {
    "index": 96,
    "speaker": "Brandon Clymer",
    "text": "He tells me these things and we, I have two choices, right.",
    "filters": {
      "metric": "He tells me these things and we, I have two choices, right.",
      "text_cleanup": "He tells me these things and we, I have two choices, right.",
      "sentiment": "neutral"
    }
  },
  {
    "index": 97,
    "speaker": "Brandon Clymer",
    "text": "I can call a meeting like this and we can address them and say again, like good, bad, indifferent.",
    "filters": {
      "task": "I can call a meeting like this and we can address them and say again, like good, bad, indifferent.",
      "text_cleanup": "I can call a meeting like this and we can address them and say again, like good, bad, indifferent.",
      "sentiment": "positive"
    }
  },
  {
    "index": 99,
    "speaker": "Brandon Clymer",
    "text": "How do we prevent this from happening and over deliver to the client?",
    "filters": {
      "question": "How do we prevent this from happening and over deliver to the client?",

      "sentiment": "negative"
    }
  },
  {
    "index": 102,
    "speaker": "Brandon Clymer",

    "filters": {
      "metric": "And the option we will do option two is I say okay, thanks and then that's it.",
      "text_cleanup": "And the option we will do option two is I say okay, thanks and then that's it.",
      "sentiment": "positive"
    }
  },
  {
    "index": 104,
    "speaker": "Brandon Clymer",
    "text": "They got, their plan is 35 buildings over the next three years.",
    "filters": {
      "metric": "They got, their plan is 35 buildings over the next three years.",
      "text_cleanup": "They got, their plan is 35 buildings over the next three years.",
      "sentiment": "neutral"
    }
  },
  {
    "index": 105,
    "speaker": "Brandon Clymer",
    "text": "They have five right now, so that's 30 buildings.",

      "text_cleanup": "They have five right now, so that's 30 buildings.",
      "sentiment": "neutral"
    }
  },
  {
    "index": 106,
    "speaker": "Brandon Clymer",
    "text": "They want to do 10 buildings every year for the next three years.",
    "filters": {
      "metric": "They want to do 10 buildings every year for the next three years.",
      "text_cleanup": "They want to do 10 buildings every year for the next three years.",
      "sentiment": "positive"
    }
  },
  {
    "index": 109,
    "speaker": "Brandon Clymer",
    "text": "Each one of these is going to be anywhere from 10 to 30 million.",

      "sentiment": "neutral"
    }
  },
  {
    "index": 113,
    "speaker": "Brandon Clymer",
    "text": "And it won't happen 10 every year.",
    "filters": {

      "text_cleanup": "And it won't happen 10 every year.",
      "sentiment": "neutral"
    }
  },
  {
    "index": 114,
    "speaker": "Brandon Clymer",
    "text": "So that means it'll go five or seven and they'll spread them out like.",
    "filters": {

      "sentiment": "positive"
    }
  },
  {
    "index": 116,
    "speaker": "Brandon Clymer",
    "text": "So when Steve takes the time to tell me his, his grievances, which is very, very much appreciative because he does not have to do that.",
    "filters": {

      "text_cleanup": "So when Steve takes the time to tell me his, his grievances, which is very, very much appreciative because he does not have to do that.",
      "sentiment": "neutral"
    }
  },
  {
    "index": 119,
    "speaker": "Brandon Clymer",
    "text": "And so that way we can make sure that we're, we're over delivering on everything.",
    "filters": {

      "text_cleanup": "And so that way we can make sure that we're, we're over delivering on everything.",
      "sentiment": "neutral"
    }
  },
  {
    "index": 122,
    "speaker": "Brandon Clymer",
    "text": "So, so the, so the biggest thing right, is, is, you know, questions coming out urgent and detailed.",

      "text_cleanup": "So, so the, so the biggest thing right, is, is, you know, questions coming out urgent and detailed.",
      "sentiment": "negative"
    }
  },
  {

    "speaker": "Brandon Clymer",

  },

      "text_cleanup": "Like, we need to make.",
      "sentiment": "positive"
    }
  },
  {
    "index": 154,
    "speaker": "Brandon Clymer",
    "text": "The first thing I said when we started this call was, hey, the Word document that you sent out was good.",

      "text_cleanup": "The first thing I said when we started this call was, hey, the Word document that you sent out was good.",

  },

      "sentiment": "positive"

  },

    "filters": {

  },

      "text_cleanup": "And I think that's kind of more the communication that you got accustomed to with Brandon and myself, you know?",
      "sentiment": "positive"
    }
  },
  {

  },

  },

  },

      "text_cleanup": "Right?",
      "sentiment": "neutral"

  },

    }
  },
  {

    "speaker": "Brandon Clymer",
    "text": "What I care about is the way that we're communicating and we're representing and, you know, if I gotta call Dan and be like, hey, call me before you call Steve, don't do this shit.",

      "text_cleanup": "What I care about is the way that we're communicating and we're representing and, you know, if I gotta call Dan and be like, hey, call me before you call Steve, don't do this shit.",
      "sentiment": "neutral"
    }
  },
  {

    "speaker": "Brandon Clymer",
    "text": "Cause I've worked with Dan before, right?",
    "filters": {

      "sentiment": "neutral"

  },

  },

  },

      "sentiment": "positive"

  },

      "text_cleanup": "That we have, please?",

  },
  {

    "text": "And I sent him a three week look ahead this morning.",

  },

```text
This document is an internal email thread discussing a potential replacement for Notion-based credit card coding and transaction processing. It is not a formal spec or report, but rather a decision-making conversation among Alleato Group leaders: Misty Rogers, Brandon Clymer, Jesse Dawson, and Maria Calcetero. The main purpose is to evaluate Bill.com Spend and Expense as an alternative tool for managing credit card transactions and syncing them into QuickBooks Online (QBO), with the goal of reducing or eliminating manual entry.

Misty Rogers proposes Bill.com Spend and Expense, highlighting that it has strong credit card transaction record-keeping capabilities and integrates directly with QBO. She also notes that she has already set up this system for multiple companies, suggesting implementation should be straightforward. She includes a YouTube training link and offers to request a quick demo from Bill.com so the team can see the platform in action. Brandon Clymer responds by asking about cost, showing that pricing is a key consideration before moving forward. Jesse Dawson then asks an important operational question: whether the system allows the company to keep its current cards or requires switching to cards issued by Bill.com.

The key details include the email dates and timeline: Misty’s proposal was sent on Thursday, July 9, 2026 at 9:55 AM; Brandon followed up at 12:49 PM with a cost question; and Jesse replied at 18:42:37Z asking about card compatibility. The thread also identifies the business context as Alleato Group, with locations in Indianapolis, IN and St. Petersburg, FL mentioned in the signatures. No final decision is made in the thread; the next steps are to determine pricing, confirm whether existing cards can be retained, and potentially schedule a Bill.com demo to evaluate the platform before adoption.
```

```text
This document is an email thread about coordinating scope and approval for the “UQ - Philipsburg - Day1 Vs Day2” work, specifically related to adding or coordinating a new fire alarm (FA) panel without disrupting Skypod operations. It is not a formal contract or report, but a project coordination and pricing communication between Alleato Group, TPG Architecture, and UNIQLO/Fast Retailing representatives. The central purpose is to clarify the work plan, confirm operational constraints, and provide pricing and documentation needed for approval.

The thread covers three main topics: operational sequencing, cost breakdown, and approval/closeout follow-up. On July 4, Kebba Mass stated the installation would be completed without disrupting Skypod operations, with one additional day needed afterward to disconnect the Skypod from the main panel and reconnect it to the new subpanel. On July 6, Yusuke Nakanishi emphasized that any disruption to Skypod was unacceptable and requested “option-B” if needed. Ahmed Elkarrimy then explained that TPG/HNY/AUE are not contracted to coordinate the new FA panel, but asked Alleato to provide a more detailed cost breakdown for leadership review and to confirm when a response from UQ is needed before the change order affects cost and schedule.

The most concrete pricing details were provided by Kebba Mass on July 8, along with a revised schedule. Kebba also noted that the revised schedule was attached and followed up on July 9 to say the updated CO with the requested cost breakdown and general conditions had been sent.

Next steps in the thread include: UQ/Yusuke reviewing the cost breakdown and granting formal approval to proceed; Alleato/Brandon and Kebba continuing coordination on the FA panel scope; and TPG requesting punch list closeout responses for MEP and architectural items, including a closeout report with photos documenting each addressed item. This document is an email thread about a request for quotation (RFQ) for a fire sprinkler project in Pensacola, FL. It is not a formal spec or contract; rather, it is a project pricing and coordination conversation between Alleato Group and Core & Main Fire Protection regarding sprinkler pipe fabrication and loose materials. The thread shows the back-and-forth needed to finalize an acceptable quote, including added scope questions and scheduling concerns.
```

```text
The main topics are pricing, painting requirements, and timing. On July 4, Kebba Mass requested a quote for fabricated pipes and loose materials for an upcoming project. On July 8, Lisa Wemhoff responded with pricing from Nexcom: $18,009.05 for fabrication, $12,681.66 for loose materials, and $30,690.71 total. Her quote notes that domestic materials were used, but it explicitly excluded painting. She also flagged specific item constraints: a 6x2 GX T reducer and a 6x1½ grv mt were quoted as non-stock, non-cancellable, and non-returnable items, and a drain cap plus U-bolt were sourced as import items because domestic versions were unavailable.

A key issue in the thread is painting. Kebba asked whether painting cost could be included and requested that it also be added to the loose materials quote. Blake Hunter then asked what the paint requirements are, noting that Navy bases often require mill thickness, painter certifications, specific color paint codes, and a month-long process including re-banding. He also asked when the project would ship if awarded. Alec Wehner later clarified that Kebba is aware of Blake’s concerns and asked Core & Main to provide pricing anyway so Alleato can understand its options. Alec also provided the project schedule: shipment is expected around July 20, with installation targeted to start July 27. The next step is for Core & Main to return pricing that includes painting, so the team can evaluate the full cost and feasibility.
```

```text
This document is an email thread about a **technical installation decision** for the **McLane Jazz Project**, specifically the **vertical pipe/sprinkler attachment** to Exotec rack uprights. It is not a formal spec or contract, but rather an **engineering coordination email exchange** between Alleato Group and Exotec to confirm the correct mounting method for the sprinkler support hardware.

The main topic is whether the **vertical sprinkler support 040045-A** can be mounted on the **front uprights** or must be attached to the **rear rack uprights**. Kebba Mass initially proposed using the upright flange holes for strut and pipe clamp installation. Exotec responded on **June 26, 2026** that there were two issues: the upright holes are **9 mm**, so **1/2-inch bolts are not compatible**, and the **front upright holes are not reachable** because the robot arm climbs close to them. On **July 8**, Dadano Thouk clarified that the support **cannot be fixed on a front upright** and can be fixed **only on a rear upright** with a specific profile. Kebba then agreed to use that approach and avoid the front uprights.

The thread ends with a final confirmation from **Sébastien Cabot** on **July 9, 2026**, stating that the team is **agreed on the mounting solution on the Rear Rack uprights**, using Exotec’s **standard vertical support**, referencing his earlier **June 25 email regarding weight acceptance**. Key requirements and constraints include the **9 mm hole size**, the incompatibility with **1/2-inch bolts**, the physical clearance issue with front uprights, and the need to use the **standard support only in the rear upright orientation**.

As next steps, the installation team should proceed with the **rear-upright mounting configuration** using the **Exotec-provided bracket/support**, and no further design change appears necessary based on this exchange. The email thread serves as a recorded agreement among the parties on the approved attachment orientation.
```

### DOCUMENTS | PSR-SOP-VIDEO-74866bb1-464f.pdf

Source ID: S137 = sharepoint_01AFX6IXKJGOGFCP5GBFBJRFZDMB5LUQHX
Project: Unassigned
Source at: 2026-07-09T20:24:52.750Z
URL: https://alleato.sharepoint.com/sites/AlleatoGroup/Shared%20Documents/Alleato%20Group/Alleato%20Group-Shared/SOP/By%20Department/Operations/PSR-SOP-VIDEO-74866bb1-464f.pdf

```text
This document is a transcribed working session for a **PSR SOP (Project Status Report standard operating procedure) video**, created on **April 20, 2026 at 3:30 PM**. Rather than a formal specification or contract, it is an operational walkthrough in which Jesse Dawson and Andrew Cannon review how to clean up project budgets, budgets/fee reallocations, change requests, and PSR reporting fields inside the job planner. The discussion is highly procedural and focused on how to interpret status, fix coding errors, and prepare accurate reporting for a project—apparently the **Westfield job** and related work.

A major topic is **budget cleanup and budget modifications**. Jesse explains that there is **no unallocated cost budget left**, so money can no longer be taken from that bucket and must instead come out of **fee** through a budget adjustment. They identify the job as being **short by 8,843** and then work through specific red-line items to correct coding errors. One fix involves moving **$3,493.39** from contractor fee to **Project Engineer labor**, and another corrects an incorrectly coded charge to **assistant superintendent expense** (not labor), adding **$9.60** to the proper cost code. They also discuss **permit requirements/permit fees**, with Andrew noting a change order for “September permit fees and all remaining project permit requirements,” and Jesse indicating that **$2,375** had been taken out previously, possibly incorrectly. The conversation suggests several cost codes and budget modifications may have been misclassified and need rework.

The second major theme is **change orders / change requests / PCOs**. Jesse reviews open change requests and notes that there are **five open change requests** at one point, with concern that some are still in draft even though the work is complete. One commitment CO should be changed to **approved** in the General tab, and Jesse says to “put it approved and move on” for items that are done. They reference a **$25,000** item and another open item tied to **lost and damaged items CIH**, which may still require payment. Jesse instructs Andrew to set the PSR field for open items to **2** and to add details in the notes section, since the two open change requests may represent about **$30,000 in potential cost**. The discussion also mentions generating a **PCO report** and redoing the **PCO log** for saving and tracking.

A final section covers **billing/payment reconciliation**, where Jesse and Andrew notice discrepancies between billed amounts, payments received, and remaining balances. They reference figures such as **2,000,028 payments**, **1,980,675**, **6,075**, and a balance/remaining amount around **850,000** that appears inconsistent. Jesse advises Andrew to **check with accounting the next day** because “something’s not making sense,” and they suspect a payment made for **Katie** may have been applied incorrectly, causing the reporting issue. The practical next steps are to confirm payment application with accounting, validate open change orders, update PSR counts/notes for open PCOs, and correct mis-coded budget items so the project status report reflects reality.
```

### DOCUMENTS | SOP Sub payments.pdf

Source ID: S139 = sharepoint_01AFX6IXML3D7CD4JTJ5DK3MNX3CRGO5UB
Project: Unassigned
Source at: 2026-07-09T20:24:59.198Z
URL: https://alleato.sharepoint.com/sites/AlleatoGroup/Shared%20Documents/Alleato%20Group/Alleato%20Group-Shared/SOP/By%20Department/Accounting/SOP%20Sub%20payments.pdf

```text
How to Pay Subcontractor Bills 
Alleato  Group  
 
Use this process any time we need to pay a subcontractor invoice. It explains what to 
check, who needs to approve it, and when payment can be made. 
When to use  
Follow these steps for all subcontractor invoices, progress payments, and final payments 
on Alleato projects. 
Who does what 
• Project Manager: Checks that the work was done and the invoice matches the job-
Pushes all payments to be made to ERP for processing. 
• Accounting/Accounts Payable: Checks the invoice, makes sure there is no 
discrepancy, confirms we have been paid for the project (example 1. below) for the 
corresponding time period and processes the payment. 
• Brandon/Jesse: Approves any exception if we want to pay a sub before we have been 
paid. 
What you need before paying 
• The subcontractor invoice/s that has synced from JobPlanner 
• Any backup needed to support the amount billed 
• Confirmation from the Project Manager that the work is complete or ready to bill, 
(completed through pushing items to ERP for processing) 
• Any required insurance, lien waiver, or other job documents 
• Confirmation that Alleato has been paid for that project or billing period, unless 
Jesse or Brandon approves an exception 
How to pay a subcontractor bill 
1. Subcontractor bills sync into the accounting software when they are released by the 
PMs in JobPlanner 

2. Items sync in an On Hold status. Before releasing the hold, ensure all descriptions 
are updated to reflect the Subcontract #, project name and Pay App detail.   
3. Check in JobPlanner that the invoice matches the subcontract amounts listed for 
that project. 
4. Check whether Alleato has been paid for the project or billing period. See example 1 
below 
5. If funds have been received, process the payment. 
6. If we have not been paid, do not pay the subcontractor unless Brandon or Jesse 
approves it first. 
Important payment rule 
Do not pay a subcontractor if Alleato has not been paid for that project or billing period. If 
there is a good reason to pay anyway, get approval from Brandon or Jesse first. 
If something is missing or doesn’t look right 
• Leave the invoice on hold if pricing does not match, or there is an issue that needs 
to be addressed with the PM. 
• Do not pay until the issue is cleared up. 
Keep a record 
Keep the invoice, backup, approvals, and proof of payment in the project file in the 
accounting email folders. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 

example 1 
In Accumatica click Construction-Projects- Search for the project and open the file-Click 
invoices tab, and review outstanding amounts
 
In this example only invoices before May can be paid to subs.
```

### DOCUMENTS | Sop For Accounting Email Maintenance.pdf

Source ID: S140 = sharepoint_01AFX6IXIE4WL6FKX5QREY7FFR22PXPDVL
Project: Unassigned
Source at: 2026-07-09T20:25:02.502Z
URL: https://alleato.sharepoint.com/sites/AlleatoGroup/Shared%20Documents/Alleato%20Group/Alleato%20Group-Shared/SOP/By%20Department/Accounting/Sop%20For%20Accounting%20Email%20Maintenance.pdf

```text
Email SOP  
Accounting Email Management 
Check the Accounting Inbox Regularly 
Open the accounting email throughout the day to ensure timely awareness of new messages. 
Act on Inbox Emails Immediately 
Any email that arrives in the inbox should be addressed as soon as possible. 
o If you are unable to resolve an email right away, leave it marked as unread so it remains 
visible until you can complete the required action. 
Processing Emailed Bills 
When a bill is received: 
• Determine if the item is a direct cost, or a project cost.  
o Project costs need to be entered into Job Planner by the Project Managers. 
o Direct costs are entered into Acumatica  
▪ Examples of direct costs are Rent, Insurance, Membership dues, Temporary 
employees 
Open the attachment and:  
• Check whether it has already been entered into Acumatica. 
• Check whether it is a bill that was previously paid  
If the invoice has been entered, file the email into the email folder for the appropriate project. 
If the invoice has been paid, file the email into the email folder for the appropriate project 
 
If the bill has not been entered and is not project related:  
▪ Save the bill. 
▪ Open Notion and enter the details for project manager approval. 
Follow the Notion entry process described in the next section. 
Emails Requiring Follow-Up or Research: 
Some messages may require additional information, responses from others, or extended research. 
o Do as much as you can immediately. 
o If further action is needed before resolution, ensure the email is marked as unread until the 
issue can be fully addressed. 
When replying from the accounting email inbox: 
• Use your signature or add your initials directly below the Accounting signature block. 
Use plain text initials (e.g., – MR).  
Adding initials allows internal staff, project managers, and subcontractors to quickly identify who sent each accounting 
response while still maintaining a unified department-facing email identity. 
 

Items That Can Be Filed Immediately 
The following types of emails can be filed as soon as they are opened, without further action: 
o Teams meeting notes (can just be deleted) 
o Advertisements (can just be deleted) 
o Coupa Notifications 
o Notion group attachments 
o Notes or informational items for project managers 
 
Notion Invoice processing: 
 
In Notion, add a new page  in the Pending invoice tab. Add all details including the project #, and attach 
the bill (see below).  
 
 
Add any notes needed, or info the PM might need. When the file is added the PM will get an email 
notification that they need to act on the bill. Once the PM adds the coding, enter the bill into Acumatica 
using the coding provided and change the Status to Posted.
```

```text
and match retainage amounts (in this screen shot the diƯerence is 5689.80 - 10% of the total
 
 
 
 
 
In this scenario the bill is in Acumatica and ready to pay, make a note of the retainage ready to pay.  
If there are several bills for the sub you will have to do this process for all bills. If you ﬁnd a bill that has 
been paid, but the payment doesn't show in JP , add it to JP .  
If bills and payments match for a sub, the contract is fulﬁlled and you can remove them from the list you 
will provide to the PM after your review. If payments are more than the bills, check for duplicate payments 
listed in JobPlanner. Make notes of odd things, items that didn’t sync, items still in draft  status etc. 
Continue through every sub, and provide notes to PM for actions they must take to complete the project,
```
